Full text

Abstract

Background: The Alouette test is widely used in France to assess reading performance and screen for dyslexia. Its low semantic predictability text limits contextual compensation and emphasizes word identification. However, its 2005 standardization relied on small grade-level samples, evidence on its screening properties in school-age children remains limited, and temporal changes in performance are poorly documented.

Methods: We pooled 5,118 Alouette assessment records from typically developing French-speaking children in Grades 1-5, collected across research projects between 2004 and 2025. We examined grade and sex differences and associations with school socioeconomic status. Screening properties were evaluated in 744 children with a prior diagnosis of developmental dyslexia and 744 matched controls. Temporal associations were examined using regression models accounting for grade, sex, and school socioeconomic status, with school-level clustering addressed where appropriate.

Sensitivity analyses examined age and school composition, and exploratory models compared gradual trends with change around 2018, when French educational reforms placed renewed emphasis on foundational reading skills and structured phonics.

Results: Performance improved across grades on all indices except errors, with no substantial sex differences. Associations with school socioeconomic status were stronger in the upper grades.

Children with dyslexia performed more poorly on all indices. The areas under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curves were .861 for reading accuracy and .902 for reading efficiency. At grade-specific 10th-percentile thresholds, reading efficiency yielded 76.3% sensitivity and 88.6% specificity. Across assessment years, reading efficiency increased by about 0.6% per year on average, whereas accuracy declined slightly, mainly in Grades 1 and 2. Exploratory models favored a difference of approximately 9% in reading efficiency between observations collected before and after 2018 over a single linear trend, although patterns varied across indices.

This study provides extensive descriptive data on Alouette performance and evidence of its potential for dyslexia screening. The proposed thresholds require independent validation before routine clinical use. Retrospective sampling and discontinuous temporal coverage limit generalizability, and the observed post-2018 difference cannot be attributed with certainty to education reforms.

1. INTRODUCTION

Reading proficiency shapes educational attainment and later opportunities, whereas low literacy carries lasting costs for employment, health and civic participation (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?ug6IiW OECD, 2013). In France, national and international assessments indicate that a substantial proportion of students complete compulsory schooling without the reading skills required for autonomous learning (Giraudeau-Barthet, 2026). In the most recent PISA cycle, French 15-year-olds scored 456 points in reading literacy, 18 points lower than in 2022 and among the lowest scores ever recorded for the country. About one student in three, compared with 22% in 2015, performed below the baseline level of proficiency (OECD, 2026). Performance is also below the European average at the end of primary school. In PIRLS 2021, French Grade 4 students scored 514 points, below the European mean of 527 (Bret et al., 2023). These surveys, however, assess reading comprehension and say little about the word identification skills on which comprehension depends (Gough and Tunmer, 1986).

One widely used indicator of the efficiency of word identification, both in research and in national monitoring, is reading fluency: the number of words read correctly aloud in one minute, which combines speed and accuracy. These indices reflect how words are identified, a process that changes markedly as reading develops. Beginning readers rely on sublexical grapheme-phoneme conversion, a serial procedure whose cost increases with word length, whereas skilled readers access orthographic representations directly, so that length largely ceases to constrain the recognition of familiar words (Coltheart et al., 2001; Ferrand et al., 2010; Brossette et al., 2024). This transition begins in the first years of reading instruction, but not all children complete it. Among 1501 French Grade 6 students, the least fluent readers still showed a word length effect for frequent words, whereas the effect had largely disappeared in the most fluent readers, and lexical decision performance predicted fluency (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?B8IsJL Lubineau et al., 2021). Fluency thus indexes the extent to which word recognition has become automatic, a prerequisite for the development of efficient multiple-word processing skills that support sentence reading in context (Brossette et al., 2022 (Brossette et al., , 2025a)).

In France, national data on fluency are recent, and they point to difficulties that are both substantial and socially stratified. When fluency was first assessed nationally at the start of Grade 6, in September 2020, students read on average 123.6 words correctly per minute (SD = 36.3). Only 53.4% reached the 120-word threshold expected at the end of Grade 5, and 15.4% read fewer than 90 words per minute, the level expected at the end of Grade 3 (Andreu et al., 2021). The social gradient was steep: 68.4% of students in the most advantaged schools reached the 120-word threshold, against 40.2% in the least advantaged. Beyond socioeconomic inequalities, substantial geographical disparities have also been documented, with a markedly elevated risk of reading difficulties among children in French overseas departments compared with mainland France (Ducrot et al., 2025).

Identifying the children concerned early enough for intervention to be effective requires instruments that isolate word identification from the contextual and comprehension processes with which it is normally intertwined.

The Alouette test (Lefavrais, 1967 (Lefavrais, , 2005) ) rests on this principle, and it has been one of the most widely used reading tests in France for six decades. Psychologists, physicians, and speech-language pathologists use it to assess reading in children, adolescents, and adults and to screen for dyslexia, and it is a common measure in research (Olivier, 2007; Bertrand et al., 2010; Cavalli et al., 2018; Casalis et al., 2023; Champagne et al., 2024). The test is particularly popular among clinicians because it is short to administer and yields measures of reading accuracy, speed and efficiency, dimensions that are typically impaired in dyslexic readers (Sprenger-Charolles et al., 2005 , 2011; Cavalli et al., 2018; Lefèvre et al., 2021).

What distinguishes the Alouette from most reading tests is the way its text is built. Lefavrais composed it from syllables that are easy to decode from the age of seven, forming words whose meaning is often easy, sometimes deliberately difficult, arranged in grammatically simple sentences (Lefavrais, 1967). The text is surrounded by visually distracting elements and contains systematic lures. Readers who anticipate based on lexical familiarity read un brin d'osier [a sprig of willow] as un brin d'acier [a strand of steel], or l'hirondeau [the young swallow] as l'hirondelle [the swallow]; readers who anticipate based on semantic plausibility read les cordeaux [the cords] as les cadeaux [the gifts], and alouette fait un nœud [the lark ties a knot] as alouette fait un nid [the lark builds a nest]. Because anticipation is penalized rather than rewarded, performance cannot rely on contextual compensation, a strategy on which weaker decoders depend more heavily (Stanovich, 1980) and which can mask decoding difficulties when the text is meaningful. Readers must instead identify each word from its orthographic form, a constraint Lefavrais presented as the condition of the test's sensitivity at all ages (Lefavrais, 1967).

The 1967 edition of the manual reports a standardization sample of 800 children from the final year of kindergarten to Grade 8. Scoring relied on three raw measures: reading time (TL), number of words read (M) and number of errors (E). The number of words read, minus the words not read, gave an apparent score, which was then adjusted for the number of errors to obtain a real score. This real score was converted into a reading age, commonly referred to as the lexical age, expressed in years and months and associated with an equivalent school grade (Lefavrais, 1967). The 2005 revision, the Alouette-R, kept the text and administration instructions unchanged but abandoned this reading-age framework. Instead, it shifted the focus to reading speed and the characterization of reading difficulties (Lefavrais, 2005). Three indices were added accordingly: the number of words read correctly (C = M -E), an accuracy index (CM = C/M × 100) and an efficiency index that accounts for both the number of words read correctly and reading time (CTL = C × 180/TL). Like fluency measures, CTL combines speed and accuracy; unlike them, it is computed on a text that prevents contextual compensation, and therefore primarily captures the word identification component of reading fluency.

Despite six decades of use, several questions remain open. The first concerns the size of the standardization sample. The Alouette-R was standardized on 398 children aged 6 to 16 from several regions of France, with only 26 to 48 children per grade from Grade 1 to Grade 5 and no information on the socioeconomic composition of the sample (Lefavrais, 2005). Samples of this size limit the precision of the resulting estimates (Oosterhuis et al.https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?TxOSma 2016) , as the manual itself acknowledges when it attributes an unexpected increase in reading time between Grade 7 and Grade 8 to a sampling bias and the limited size of the sample (Lefavrais, 2005, p.17). A larger sample would allow performance to be characterized more precisely and would make it possible to examine how performance relates to factors that small samples cannot accommodate, such as the socioeconomic composition of schools. A second question concerns the use of the test for screening. Its sensitivity, that is, its ability to correctly identify children with dyslexia, and its specificity, which is the ability to correctly identify children without dyslexia, have been established in adults (Cavalli et al., 2018) but not in school-age children. Estimating them across a range of cut-off values (Lalkhen and McCluskey, 2008) would clarify what the test can contribute to a comprehensive assessment of reading difficulties. A third question concerns changes over time. Children's reading habits have changed considerably as digital media have taken a growing place in their daily lives: children in France spend on average three hours a day in front of screens, against 18 minutes reading (Tétaz and Leray, 2026). How reading performance has evolved alongside these changes remains poorly documented, in part because data collected with the same procedure over long periods are rare. One exception comes from the United States. Spichtig et al. (2016) compared the silent reading rate, measured under a comprehension criterion, of 2203 students in Grades 2 to 12 with that of a cohort assessed half a century earlier (Taylor, 1965). Rates were similar in Grade 2 (115 and 116 words per minute) but diverged from Grade 4 onwards, increasingly with grade, reaching a deficit of 38 words per minute in Grade 8 and a 19% decline by the end of secondary school (192 against 237 words per minute; Hedges' g = 0.81). Students in the recent cohort also made more fixations per word at every grade, which the authors interpreted as reduced automaticity of word recognition. Oral reading fluency norms suggest the opposite trend, with a gain of about five words correct per minute on average across grades between 2006 and 2017 (Hasbrouck and Tindal, 2017). These norms, however, average scores from several commercial assessments, and the assessments and samples differed between the two compilations. The apparent gain may therefore reflect differences in the texts and populations assessed, as well as changes in children's reading performance.

In France, these questions have taken on a policy dimension. The highly centralized French educational system places schools at the forefront of reading development, making educational policies particularly relevant (Brossette et al., 2025b). In 2018, official guidelines called for systematic teaching of grapheme-phoneme correspondences in Grade 1 and for regular reading-aloud practice to develop fluency, with fluent oral reading expected by the end of Grade 3 (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?Z7unfq. In the same periodhttps://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?8kvJT7 class sizes in Grades 1 and 2 were halved in priority education schools, national standardized assessments were introduced at the beginning of Grades 1 and 2 (Evrard et al., 2025) , and a fluency test was added to the Grade 6 assessment in 2020 (Andreu et al., 2021). Expected fluency thresholds were set in 2019 at 50, 70, 90, 110 and 120 words read correctly per minute at the end of Grades 1 to 5, respectively (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?vXk4C7. National monitoring has since reported progress, but little change among the weakest readers: the proportion of students entering Grade 6 who reached the 120-word threshold rose from 53% in 2021 to 61.3% in 2025, whereas the proportion reading fewer than 90 words per minute barely moved, from 15.4% in 2020 to 14.9% in 2025 (Andreu et al., 2021; Ballereau et al., 2026). Because these data begin with the reforms, they cannot show whether performance after 2018 departs from the trajectory that preceded them.

The present study had three objectives. The first was descriptive: to characterize performance on the Alouette in a large dataset of assessments from children attending Grades 1 to 5 (N = 5118), and to examine how performance on each index varies with grade and sex and relates to the socioeconomic composition of schools. Given the size of the dataset, these data provide a useful resource for researchers and practitioners working with French-speaking children. The second objective was to estimate the sensitivity and specificity of the test by comparing 744 children with a prior diagnosis of developmental dyslexia with 744 typically developing children from the dataset, with similar distributions of grade, sex and age, and to identify cut-off scores that may be useful for screening pending independent validation. The third objective was to examine how performance has changed over time, in a context of recurring debates about a possible decline in reading skills (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?jyHjUE Desmurget, 2023). Because it pools data collected between 2004 and 2025 across successive research projects, the dataset makes it possible to describe how reading efficiency and accuracy have varied over two decades, while taking into account changes in grade composition and in the socioeconomic profile of schools. Such data complement international surveys, which target comprehension, and national monitoring, which began with the 2018 reforms. These reforms targeted decoding and fluency directly. The Alouette therefore provides a relevant measure for exploring reading performance around this period. As the dataset includes assessments conducted both before and after 2018, it also allows an exploratory examination of whether temporal differences in performance are better described by a gradual trend or by a change around this period.

Together, the analyses provide descriptive reference information, evaluate the test's discriminative properties in a case-control sample, and explore temporal variation in performance, while recognizing the different inferential scope of each approach.

2. METHOD

>METHOD

2.1. Participants

The present study draws on data assembled retrospectively. We pooled data from successive research This process yielded 5118 Alouette test results from typically developing French-speaking children with no diagnosed developmental difficulties (see Table 1). For each test, we recorded the child's grade, chronological age, sex, year of assessment, school, and school Social Position Index 1 (SPI; indice de position sociale, IPS, in French) (Rocher, 2016 (Rocher, , 2023)). Because the data were aggregated in anonymized form, we could not determine whether the same child had been tested in different research projects over several years. Each test was treated as an independent observation. All children had normal or corrected-to-normal vision, and none had any known neurological, psychiatric, or emotional disorder. These research projects also provided 744 Alouette test results from children with developmental dyslexia (DD). The diagnosis was made prior to the test, in reference centers for learning disorders or 1 The Social Position Index is a composite index based on occupational and socioprofessional categories of students' parents. Each category, or combination of parental categories, is assigned a score summarizing socioeconomic and cultural characteristics associated with educational achievement. A school's SPI corresponds to the average SPI of its students. The IPS provides a measure of socioeconomic inequalities within the French school system. IPS values in the sample ranged from 86.2 to 148.4 (national mean = 104.6; national range = 38-179). No very low-SES schools (IPS < 81) were represented in the sample. in private speech-language therapy practices. Because children were recruited across several clinical settings, the diagnostic instruments varied between centers, although all applied comparable psychometric criteria based on standardized reading measures. Diagnoses were established using one of the following batteries: the ODEDYS-2 (Jacquier-Roux et al.https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?W8Y5Sz 2002) https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?Y6ryZi with reading speed and accuracy scores for isolated irregular words and pseudowords at least 1.5 SD below the mean of same-age average readers; the EVALEC (Sprenger-Charolles et al.https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?e8Rfto 2005) , with pseudoword scores more than 1.65 SD below those of same-age average readers; or the BALE (Jacquier-Roux et al., 2010) , with pseudoword or irregular word accuracy and reading fluency scores more than 1.5 SD below those of same-age average readers. Children with an intellectual developmental disorder were excluded (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?cHYFpz Wechsler, 2005). All diagnoses were made by medical or paramedical professionals on the basis of the batteries listed above, without reference to the Alouette test.

2.2. L'Alouette text

The 265-word text of L'Alouette evokes a pastoral and poetic world of birds, springtime, and natural landscapes. Birds nest, sing, and play among trees and gardens. As the text progresses, the initially peaceful scene takes on a more mysterious quality. The text is printed in varying font sizes and accompanied by illustrations designed to elicit contextual reading errors.

Several features make L'Alouette particularly useful for assessing reading fluency in young readers.

Its vocabulary includes both common words and less familiar or unusual expressions, such as écaille argentée ("silvery scale") and brignoles perfides ("treacherous brignoles"), thereby allowing the assessment of readers' ability to decode unfamiliar words. Unlike a conventional narrative, the text has no clear beginning, middle, or end; instead, its sentences follow one another with little or no logical connection. Some constructions also depart from ordinary linguistic usage, reflecting the freedom of poetic language. Examples include Le printemps a nids au bois ("Spring has nests in the woods") and Au bois joli gamine le pinson ("In the lovely woods, the chaffinch playsfoot_0 "). These features help assess young readers' decoding efficiency by limiting the extent to which reading can rely on syntactic regularities and contextual cues. Finally, the text contains repetitions and sequences of words with similar phonological forms, such as Annie amie and amie Annie, which may induce reading errors and thus provide an indication of decoding accuracy.

2.3. Test administration and scoring

The children were recruited from different settings. Typically developing (TD) children were tested in 49 mainstream schools. Children with developmental dyslexia were tested in clinical and specialized settings, including hospital departments, specialized classes for children with disabilities, and specialized care facilities for children with neurodevelopmental and learning disorders.

The Alouette test was administered individually to all children following standardized procedures.

Assessments were conducted in a quiet room, either within the child's school or, for children recruited through hospital services or specialized care facilities, within the corresponding institution.

All assessments were conducted under consistent natural lighting conditions. Before data collection, all examiners received standardized training in test administration and scoring procedures. Typed instruction scripts were used and read aloud verbatim to all participants.

Before seating the child, the examiner prepared the stopwatch, ensuring that the child remained unaware that reading time was being measured. The examiner placed the illustrated text sheet in front of the child without any instructions other than the standardized prompt, "Here, read all of this," while pointing with a pencil to the text from the title to the end. The stopwatch was discreetly started as soon as the child began reading. Reading time was limited to 3 minutes. If the child completed the text in under 3 minutes, the examiner recorded the reading time on the recording sheet.

If the child paused for more than 5 seconds on a word, the examiner said, "Leave that one and continue." If the child still did not proceed, the examiner repeated the instruction while pointing to the next word and saying, "Read this one." Throughout the task, the examiner circled the graphemes corresponding to reading errors on the recording sheet. Each misread word, isolated word omission, skipped line, or word not read within 5 seconds was counted as one error. Spontaneously self-corrected errors were not counted.

Three measures were recorded: reading time (TL, in seconds, capped at 180 s), number of words read (M), and number of errors (E). Three indices were derived from them: the number of words read correctly (C = M -E), an accuracy index (CM = C/M × 100), and an efficiency index (CTL = C × 180/TL), which combines speed and accuracy. Note that for children who did not finish the text within 180 s, TL = 180 and CTL therefore equals C, which may limit the index's sensitivity to reading speed in slower readers (typically Grade 1 and 2 children). See the Discussion for implications for the present results. The statistical analyses presented below were based on five measures: reading time (TL), words read (M), words read correctly (C), accuracy (CM), and the composite efficiency index (CTL).

3. RESULTS

>RESULTS

3.1. Descriptive performance data by grade

Alouette test performance for each index by grade is provided in Table 2. For each index, we examined whether performance varied according to sex, grade, and the school's social position index.

Non-parametric statistical methods were used, as Shapiro-Wilk tests indicated that the distributions of all indices deviated significantly from normality. For sex, we ran a Kruskal-Wallis test on each index, and we repeated the test within each grade with Holm correction across grades. For grade, we also ran a Kruskal-Wallis test on each index, followed by Dunn post-hoc comparisons with Holm correction. We used eta-squared (η²) as the effect size. Finally, Spearman correlations between each index and the school Social Position Index were computed. Sex. Sex had little meaningful effect. The overall test was nonsignificant for all indices except TL, for which the effect was negligible (p = .013; η² = .001; all others ps > .11). The within-grade analyses confirmed this pattern: none of the 30 comparisons remained significant after Holm correction (smallest adjusted p = .071). These findings are consistent with Lefavrais (2005) https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?49lEhi who likewise found no significant sex differences on any of the Alouette indices in his sample of 398 children. Accordingly, the analyses are reported below without stratifying by sex.

Grade. Means and standard deviations by grade are reported in Table 2. Grade had a strong effect on all six indices (all ps < .001). Effect sizes were very large for M, C, and CTL (all η² > .53), large for CM (η² = .33) and TL (η² = .25), and small for E (η² = .04). Dunn comparisons revealed significant differences between all grades on all indices (all ps < .001), except for E between Grades 4 and 5, for which the difference approached statistical significance (p = .053). Lefavrais (2005) also reported significant age-related differences in Alouette performance, but only for specific indices and grade transitions: TL and CM between Grades 2 and 3 (ages 7-8), and the number of words read and correctly read between and between Grades 10 and 11 (ages 15-16).

Social Position Index (SPI).

At the overall level, correlations were significant but negligible: TL (ρ = -.09), E (ρ = -.05), and M (ρ = -.03) were weakly related to SPI (all ps < .05), while C, CM, CTL, and CWPM were not (all ps > .05). A developmental pattern emerged when correlations were examined by grade. No index was related to SPI in Grade 1 (all ps > .35). Weak associations appeared in Grade 2 (TL: ρ = -.14; CTL and CWPM: ρ = +.06) and Grade 3 (E: ρ = -.14; CM: ρ = +.13). By Grades 4 and 5, all indices were associated with SPI (all ps < .001), most strongly reading efficiency (CTL: ρ = +.24 and +.21; CWPM: ρ = +.24 and +.21) and reading time (TL: ρ = -.28 and -.22). Associations between school socioeconomic composition and reading performance were thus more pronounced in the upper grades than in the lower grades. As our sample was socially advantaged relative to the national school population, its raw means could overstate the performance expected at the national level. To assess how much this affected the descriptive values, we added an SPI-weighted sensitivity analysis based on post-stratification. Schools were classified into the four quartiles of the SPI distribution among the 29,070 metropolitan French schools recorded for 2024-2025 (SPI < 95.5, 95.5-<104.8, 104.8-<114.8, and ≥ 114.8; Rocher, 2023). Each quartile received a weight equal to the ratio of its share in the national school population to its share among the participating schools. This weight was applied to every child attending a school in that quartile.

We then compared the weighted and unweighted estimates. Weighting left most estimates essentially unchanged. For most indices and grades, the weighted and unweighted means differed by less than two points. The differences were concentrated in Grade 4 and Grade 5, and only for the indices most strongly tied to SPI. There, the weighted means were slightly lower: reading efficiency (CTL) by about 9 to 11 points (-3 to -4%) and reading time (TL) rose by about 3 seconds. Accuracy (CM) and error counts (E) were unaffected (differences below 0.7 points). Unweighted values are reported in Table 2, weighted values in Appendix B, and exploratory grade-specific percentiles for clinical use, both unweighted and weighted, in Appendix C.

3.2. Screening properties of the Alouette test.

>Screening properties of the Alouette test.

3.2.1. Data processing and analysis.

Finally, we examined whether Alouette performance could distinguish children with developmental dyslexia (DD) from typically developing (TD) readers. Performance was compared between the 744 children with DD and 744 TD children selected from our cohort to match the DD group on grade distribution, sex distribution, and overall mean chronological age (see Table 3). Independent-samples t tests with Cohen's d were used for comparability with previous studies of the Alouette test. Given the equal group sizes, the conventional t test was retained; Welch's correction yielded identical results. Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve analyses were then performed on two indices, reading efficiency (CTL), which combines speed and accuracy, and reading accuracy (CM), to assess their ability to differentiate DD from TD children. For each index, the area under the curve (AUC) was computed as an index of discriminative ability (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?PQf60q and optimal cut-off scores were identified using the Youden index, which maximizes the sum of sensitivity and specificity.

Sensitivity and specificity were additionally computed at the 10th and 5th percentiles of the unweighted sample distribution, which are commonly used as referral thresholds in clinical practice.

3.2.2. Descriptive results.

Children with DD performed more poorly than TD readers on all six Alouette measures assessed in this comparison (all ps < .001), with large effect sizes throughout (all |d| values > 0.8) except for the number of errors (d = 0.63). The distributions of the reading efficiency index are particularly illustrative in this respect (Figure 1): although the two groups overlap, they are clearly distinct, with median scores of 253 in TD readers and 98.0 in children with DD. These descriptive data, however, do not indicate from which score a child should be considered at risk of dyslexia, which is what clinical practice requires. We therefore used ROC curve analysis (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?SLQw4w) to estimate sensitivity and specificity for the two indices, from which screening cut-off scores can be derived.

3.2.3. Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) Analysis.

In ROC analysis, the area under the curve (AUC) provides a probabilistic index of discriminative ability: the probability that a randomly selected TD child obtains a higher score than a randomly selected child with DD. AUC values range from .50 (chance) to 1.0 (perfect discrimination). ROC curves were computed for the CTL and CM indices, allowing their discriminative power to be compared (Figure 2). The AUC was good for CM (.861, SE = .010, 95% CI [.842, .880], p < .001) and excellent for CTL (.902, SE = .008, 95% CI [.886, .918], p < .001). CTL therefore showed a numerically higher AUC than CM, suggesting better discrimination between children with and without dyslexia in this sample. Sensitivity and specificity for the optimal cut-off and for the 10th and 5th percentiles are reported in Table 4. The optimal cut-off derived from the Youden index provides the best balance between sensitivity and specificity for a single cut-off applied across grades, correctly identifying 82.1% of children with dyslexia and 82.7% of typically developing readers for CTL (Figure 3).

Percentile-based cut-offs are also reported, as these are the thresholds most commonly used in clinical practice. They yield higher specificity, meaning that fewer children without reading difficulties would be referred, but at the cost of lower sensitivity. CTL maintained higher sensitivity at both percentile thresholds: even at the 5th percentile, two-thirds of the children with dyslexia are still detected (67.7%), compared with 54.0% for CM. Reporting these values allows clinicians to weigh sensitivity against specificity and to set referral thresholds according to the clinical context.

Children scoring below the 10th or 5th percentile may warrant further evaluation of their reading skills, particularly when other clinical information points in the same direction. However, these thresholds should be considered exploratory pending validation in independent screening samples.

3.3. Changes in reading performance between 2004 and 2025.

>Changes in reading performance between 2004 and 2025.

3.3.1. Data processing and analysis.

Changes over time were examined with regression models adapted to each Alouette index. Children were nested within 49 schools, and about 18% of the variance in CTL lay between schools (ICC = .18). Models for M, C, CM, and CTL therefore included a random intercept for school. They did not include a random slope for year. Only 24 of the 49 schools were assessed in more than one year, and only four were assessed both before and after 2018, so school-specific trends could not be estimated reliably. Assessment year was entered as a continuous predictor, centered on 2004. All models included grade, sex, and school SPI as covariates.

Each measure was modeled with a distribution suited to its properties. TL is censored at 180 s, because children who do not finish the text are stopped at that point. It was modeled with a lognormal accelerated failure time model, which treats 180 s as a censoring point rather than an observed time. This model did not allow random effects. Standard errors were therefore made robust to clustering by school, and a model with school fixed effects was fitted as a sensitivity analysis.

Effects on TL are expressed as time ratios (TRs), and a TR below 1 means a shorter reading time. M and C are counted out of the 265 words of the text. They were modeled with beta-binomial distributions, which take this upper limit into account and allow for overdispersion. CM was modeled in the same way, as the number of words read correctly out of the number of words read.

Effects on M, CM, and C are expressed as odds ratios (ORs). CTL is positive and right-skewed, and its variance increases with its level. It was modeled with a lognormal distribution, and yearly effects are expressed as ratios. A ratio of 1.006 means that scores were multiplied by 1.006 each year, an increase of 0.6%. For each measure, we compared candidate models that differed in the distribution assumed for the outcome, using the Akaike information criterion (AIC), which evaluates how well a model fits the data while penalizing its complexity (the number of parameters). We retained the model with the lowest AIC, which represents the best compromise between fit and parsimony.

Ratios and ORs describe change on the scale of the model. On that scale, the same ratio or OR corresponds to different absolute changes depending on the starting level. To make the estimates easier to interpret, we also computed average marginal effects of year for each grade. These effects express the yearly change in the original unit of each measure: points for CTL, words for M and C, and percentage points for CM. For TL, they express the change in the probability of finishing the text within 180 s, because reading times are not observed for children who do not finish. They were computed from the fixed effects and averaged over the children of each grade.

For each measure, we tested whether the yearly trend differed across grades by adding an interaction term between year and grade (likelihood-ratio test). The interaction was retained when significant, and grade-specific slopes were then compared with Tukey-adjusted pairwise contrasts. Moreover, three robustness checks were carried out. First, we separated the year effect within schools assessed in several years from the year effect between schools assessed at different dates. This tests whether the trend merely reflects changes in which schools were sampled. Second, we added children's age, centered within grade, to test whether a drift in age at testing could explain the trend. Third, for the multilevel models, we allowed residual dispersion to vary by grade. Although this improved model fit, the estimated temporal effects remained largely unchanged. In 2018, French national guidelines on reading instruction and primary school reforms refocused teaching on foundational skills, including structured phonics. This date therefore provides a plausible reference point for examining whether performance changed over the period in a way that a steady linear trend would not capture.

Because no data were collected in 2018, the break lies between the last assessments before the reforms (2017) and the first assessments after them (2019). The analysis is presented as exploratory.

All analyses were conducted in R (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?Xh25Jm. Mixed-effects models were fitted with glmmTMB (Brooks et al., 2017). The censored model for TL was fitted with the survreg function of the survival package (Therneau, 2024). Grade-specific slopes and pairwise contrasts were estimated with emmeans (Lenthhttps://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?s2Aa5Q 2025) , and average marginal effects with marginaleffects (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?PEH4TG. Model fit was checked with simulation-based residuals from DHARMa (Hartig, 2016).

The summary of results is presented in Table 5.

We first present the three elementary measures (reading time (TL), words read (M), and words read correctly (C)), then accuracy (CM), and finally the composite index (CTL). See Table 5 for a synthesis.

3.3.2. Reading time (TL)

Reading time is capped at 180 s. Overall, 73.4% of children (3,759 of 5,118) did not finish the text within this limit, from 98.6% in Grade 1 and 90.6% in Grade 2 to 42.0% in Grade 5. Despite this censoring, reading time decreased by about 1.4% per year (TR = 0.986, 95% CI [0.977, 0.995]). The trend did not differ across grades (χ²(4) = 4.58, p = .33). However, this test has limited power because more than 90% of children in Grades 1 and 2 did not finish the text within 180 s. With school fixed effects, the estimated decline was steeper (TR = 0.958 per year), suggesting that changes in school composition may have attenuated the long-term trend observed in the main model.

Importantly, the decline remained evident when comparisons were restricted to within-school variation.

Adjusting for age did not change the estimate. In original units, the probability of finishing the text within 180 s increased by 1.76 percentage points per year in Grade 4 (95% CI [0.64, 2.63]) and by 1.73 in Grade 5 (95% CI [0.62, 2.66]). The gain was smaller in Grade 1 (0.18 percentage points per year), where only 1.4% of children finished the text.

3.3.3. Words read (M)

The number of words read increased by about 1.4% per year on the odds scale (OR = 1.014, 95% CI [1.005, 1.023]). The trend did not differ across grades (χ²(4) = 6.00, p = .20). It was similar within schools (OR = 1.012, 95% CI [1.002, 1.023]) and between schools (OR = 1.019, 95% CI [1.002, 1.036]; difference: p = .53). Older children within a grade read more words (OR = 1.013 per month, 95% CI [1.006, 1.021]), but adjusting for age did not change the year effect.

In original units, the gain decreased with grade. M increased by 0.87 words per year in Grade 1 (95% CI [0.34, 1.40]) and by 0.20 words per year in Grade 5 (95% CI [0.08, 0.33]). Under the common-slope model, this difference largely reflects the ceiling of the text: 53% of Grade 5 children read all 265 words. However, the assumption of a common slope warrants some caution. In a sensitivity model allowing both a year × grade interaction and grade-specific residual dispersion, the interaction estimates for Grades 3 and 5 were below 1, with confidence intervals excluding 1 (OR = 0.981, 95% CI [0.964, 0.998], and OR = 0.977, 95% CI [0.958, 0.996], respectively). These findings suggest that the increase in M may have been weaker in these grades than in Grade 1, although this was not established by the global interaction test in the primary model. In the upper grades, the ceiling of the text also limits the ability of M to capture further gains.

3.3.4. Words read correctly (C)

The index C combines the number of words read and accuracy. It increased over the period (OR = 1.011, 95% CI [1.004, 1.017]). Thus, the increase in the number of words read (M) was accompanied by an overall increase in correctly read words (C). This occurred despite the slight decline in accuracy in the lower grades, described below. The trend did not differ across grades (χ²(4) = 3.14, p = .53). It was similar within schools (OR = 1.010, 95% CI [1.003, 1.018]) and between schools (OR = 1.011, 95% CI [1.000, 1.022]; difference: p = .92). Adjusting for age did not change the year effect.

In original units, C increased by 0.61 words per year in Grade 1 (95% CI [0.26, 0.97]) and by 0.35 words per year in Grade 5 (95% CI [0.15, 0.55]).

3.3.5. Accuracy (CM)

Unlike TL, M and C, the trend in CM differed across grades (χ²(4) = 23.30, p < .001). Accuracy decreased slightly in Grade 1 (OR = 0.985, 95% CI [0.979, 0.991]) and in Grade 2 (OR = 0.977, 95% CI [0.969, 0.984]).

In original units, this corresponds to a decrease of about 0.2 percentage points per year in both grades (Grade 1: -0.20, 95% CI [-0.27, -0.12]; Grade 2: -0.22, 95% CI [-0.29, -0.15]). The decline was smaller in Grade 4 (OR = 0.992, 95% CI [0.98, 0.99], p < .05; -0.05 percentage points per year) and was not significant in Grades 3 (OR = 0.993, 95% CI [0.98, 1.00], p = .118) and 5 (OR = 0.998, 95% CI [0.99, 1.01], p = .717).

The Grade 2 slope was steeper than those of Grades 3, 4 and 5 (all ps < .01). The trend was similar within and between schools (p = .35) and did not change after adjusting for age.

3.3.6. Reading efficiency (CTL)

CTL increased over the period. Averaged across grades, each additional year was associated with an increase of about 0.6% (ratio = 1.006, 95% CI [1.004, 1.009], p < .001). This trend did not simply reflect changes in which schools were sampled. The association was similar within schools (ratio = 1.007, 95% CI [1.005, 1.010]) and between schools (ratio = 1.006, 95% CI [1.001, 1.011]), and the two estimates did not differ (p = .67). Adjusting for age within grade left the estimate unchanged.

The trend differed across grades (χ² (4) = 20.55, p < .001). Yearly gains were 0.7% in Grade 1, 0.5% in Grade 4, and 1.3% in Grade 5. In Grades 2 and 3, the slopes were about 0.3% and did not differ from 0 (p = .21 and .17). The Grade 5 slope was steeper than those of Grades 2, 3, and 4 (all ps < .01). It did not differ from the Grade 1 slope (p = .23), and no other pair of grades differed. In original units, CTL increased by 0.68 points per year in Grade 1 (95% CI [0.25, 1.11]) and by 3.39 points per year in Grade 5 (95% CI [2.41, 4.36]). In Grade 1, the yearly gain in CTL was numerically close to that of C (0.68 points vs. 0.61 words), consistent with the fact that CTL equals C when the text is not completed within 180 seconds. In Grade 5, the yearly gain in CTL was substantially larger than that in C (3.39 points vs. 0.35 words), suggesting that changes in reading time and text completion also contributed to the observed increase in CTL.

3.3.7. Exploratory analysis: gradual trend or change around 2018.

We compared three forms of the trend with AIC: a linear trend, a level shift in 2018, and a change in slope at 2018. The breakpoint was fixed a priori. No data were collected in 2018, so the last assessments before the break were conducted in 2017 and the first after it in 2019; 2,315 assessments were conducted before 2018 and 2,803 afterwards. We then re-estimated the 2018 change in the four schools assessed both before and after 2018. For TL, it was re-estimated in Grades 4 and 5, where most reading times are observed.

For CTL, a level shift at 2018 described the data better than a linear trend (ΔAIC = 8.4). Scores were about 9% higher after 2018 than before (ratio = 1.088), with no clear evidence of a slope within either period. A difference of similar size was found within the four schools assessed on both sides of 2018, when comparison was restricted to school-by-grade cells observed in both periods (+9.9%, 95% CI [6.2, 13.8]). The overall increase in CTL (see Section 3.2.6) can therefore be better described by higher scores in the recent data than by a steady progression over two decades, although this analysis is exploratory.

The components did not all change in the same way. For TL, the reduction in reading time stalled after 2018 (slope change: TR (Time Ratio) = 1.028, 95% CI [1.011, 1.045]), meaning that the yearly decline of TL was smaller after 2018. This pattern also held in Grades 4 and 5. For M, a step appeared in the full sample (OR = 1.32, 95% CI [1.08, 1.63]) but it was not significant in the four schools assessed on both sides of 2018 (OR = 1.16, 95% CI [0.89, 1.52]), and the three forms of the trend fitted the data almost equally well. The index C also showed a step (OR = 1.17, 95% CI [1.01, 1.36]). A difference of similar size was found in the four schools (OR = 1.14, 95% CI [1.03, 1.25]), but it did not hold when some individual schools were removed. For CM, the decline observed before 2018 reversed afterward (post-2018 slope: OR = 1.033, 95% CI [1.015, 1.051]). This post-2018 recovery was also observed in the schools assessed on both sides of 2018.

After 2018, higher CTL was thus accompanied by higher C and by a recovery of accuracy, whereas reading time stopped decreasing. Note. CTL = reading efficiency index; M = number of words read; TL = reading time; CM = percentage of words read correctly; C = number of words read correctly; G1 = Grade 1; G5 = Grade 5; OR = odds ratio. ᵃAveraged across grades, because the year x grade interaction was retained; ᵇIn a sensitivity model with grade-specific residual dispersion, the year x grade terms for G3 and G5 excluded 1.

3.4. Comparison with ministerial reading-fluency benchmarks

Purpose. In France, teachers and clinicians usually express reading fluency as the number of correctly read words per minute, and the Ministry of Education specifies expected end-of-year levels in this unit: 50, 70, 90, 110 and 120 words per minute at the end of Grades 1 to 5, respectively (Lubineau et al., 2021). The main index of the Alouette, CTL, is not expressed in this unit. To make our results easier to interpret in educational and clinical practice, we expressed performance in correctly read words per minute (CWPM = C × 60 / TL, i.e., CTL / 3) and compared the performance of the children of our cohort with these benchmarks. This comparison is descriptive: its aim is to situate Alouette scores in a familiar unit, not to classify children or to validate the benchmarks.

Method. CWPM was computed for each child. For children who did not finish the text within 180 seconds, it corresponds to the number of correct words divided by three. In Table 6, we report means, SDs, and medians by grade, and the benchmark of the French Ministry.

Results. At every grade, children read fewer correct words per minute on the Alouette than the end-of-year benchmarks specify: 56% of the expected value in Grade 1, and 70% to 78% in Grades 2 to 5, with absolute gaps of 21 to 26 words per minute.

Discussion. These two sets of values are not strictly comparable, because they serve different purposes and rest on different tasks. The ministerial benchmarks are end-of-year targets meant to guide teaching (Lubineau et al., 2021) rather than norms derived from observed performance, and they refer to the oral reading of meaningful texts. The norms of a test, in contrast, describe performance on a specific task in a reference sample; for the Alouette, this task is a grammatically correct but semantically meaningless text. The two should be consistent with each other, but need not be identical. In addition, our children were assessed between December and June, so that part of the sample had not reached the end of the school year to which the benchmarks apply. These comparisons should therefore not be used to classify children relative to the benchmarks on the basis of their Alouette score.

4. DISCUSSION

This study examined performance on the Alouette test in a large dataset of French primary school children, its discriminative properties for developmental dyslexia, and temporal differences in performance between 2004 and 2025. The dataset provides extensive descriptive reference information on Alouette performance across primary school grades, complementing existing normative data without constituting a formal restandardization of the test.

Main findings

The study yielded three main findings. First, Alouette performance differed substantially across primary school grades, with limited sex differences and stronger associations with school socioeconomic composition in the upper grades. Second, reading efficiency (CTL) showed promising discrimination between children with and without a prior diagnosis of developmental dyslexia, although the proposed thresholds require independent validation. Third, reading efficiency was higher in more recent observations, with exploratory analyses suggesting that a contrast between periods before and after 2018 may describe the data better than a single linear trend.

What does the CTL index really measure?

Because CTL is computed as the number of words read correctly weighted by reading time (C × 180 / TL), the relative contribution of its components varies according to whether the child completes the text. For the 73.4% of children (3,759 of 5,118) who did not finish within 180 seconds, TL is fixed and CTL strictly equals C: a count of correct words bounded by the time limit, whose time component carries no speed variation between children. For children who finish in less than 180 seconds, which was typical of Grades 4 and 5, CTL is additionally weighted by reading time and is no longer bounded by the length of the text. Formally, CTL is a rate of correct reading (correct words per 180 seconds) in both cases, but it is determined by a bounded count in the first case and by a continuous reading time in the second. CTL therefore has different measurement properties depending on text completion, which may affect comparisons across grades and help explain its differential sensitivity to long-term trends: in Grade 1, the yearly gain in CTL was close to that of C, whereas in Grade 5 it was much larger, consistent with the contribution of shorter reading times. The average trend across grades should thus be read as a descriptive summary rather than as a uniform change in the underlying components of reading efficiency. Moreover, because errors are already included in C, CTL alone cannot distinguish a slow reader from an inaccurate one, which is why M, CM and TL were analyzed separately.

Screening properties

CTL discriminated well between children with developmental dyslexia and typically developing readers matched on grade, sex and mean age (AUC = .902), with a numerically higher AUC than accuracy alone (CM; AUC = .861). The grade-specific percentile cut-offs, which were computed on the distribution of the typically developing cohort and not tuned to the dyslexia group, performed as well as a single optimal cut-off (for CTL, Youden's J was .649 at the 10th percentile and .648 at the optimal cut-off). This suggests that grade-specific thresholds, which can be derived from reference data, may preserve discriminative performance in this sample while accounting for grade-related differences in CTL distributions. At the 10th and 5th percentiles, CTL kept a higher sensitivity than CM (76.3% vs. 68.5%, and 67.7% vs. 54.0%) at similar specificity.

These results suggest that CTL carries useful information for screening, but they are exploratory.

Importantly, discrimination was evaluated in a case-control sample comparing previously diagnosed children recruited from clinical settings with typically developing controls. The observed sensitivity and specificity may therefore not generalize to screening in unselected school populations, where reading difficulties are more heterogeneous. The optimal cut-off was chosen to maximize Youden's index in the same sample in which sensitivity and specificity were estimated, which may make these estimates somewhat optimistic, although this bias decreases with sample size (Leeflang et al., 2008) and is probably small with groups of 744 children. Percentile-based cut-offs, which are not selected to maximize discrimination, are less affected. As in most studies of this kind, the diagnosis of dyslexia rests on other reading tests, which here varied across projects: their overlap with the construct measured by the Alouette may inflate discrimination, and their heterogeneity complicates the interpretation of the cut-offs. Finally, sensitivity and specificity in a balanced sample do not indicate the proportion of identified children who truly have dyslexia in a population where the disorder is less frequent. These cut-offs therefore need to be validated in independent samples before being used for clinical decisions.

Temporal trends in reading performance

Reading performance on the Alouette test was higher in the most recent years than in the early ones.

Considered as a linear trend, reading efficiency (CTL) rose by 0.6% per year after adjustment for grade, sex and school social position index (SPI), and this trend was not explained by changes in which schools were sampled or by age differences within grades. It differed across grades: gains were significant in Grades 1 (0.7% per year), 4 (0.5%) and 5 (1.3%) but not in Grades 2 and 3 (about 0.3%), and the Grade 5 slope exceeded those of Grades 2 to 4 but did not differ from that of Grade 1. In points, gains were 0.68 per year in Grade 1 and 3.39 in Grade 5, values that are not directly comparable across grades because CTL distributions and the relative contributions of its components differ across grades. Reading time (TL) decreased by 1.4% per year, and the probability of finishing the text within 180 seconds increased by 1.76 percentage points per year in Grade 4 and 1.73 in Grade 5. The numbers of words read (M) and read correctly (C) also increased, with trends that did not differ across grades on the odds scale (in Grade 1, 0.87 and 0.61 words per year; in Grade 5, 0.20 and 0.35, differences largely due to the ceiling of the text). Accuracy (CM), in contrast, declined slightly in Grades 1 and 2 (by 0.20 and 0.22 percentage points per year) and less in Grade 4 (0.05 points), and did not change significantly in Grades 3 and 5.

A possible change around 2018 (exploratory analysis)

An exploratory analysis nuanced this picture. For CTL, a model allowing a level difference between observations collected before and after 2018, the year of French education reforms, fitted the data better than a linear trend: scores were 8.8% higher after 2018, with no clear slope within either period, and a similar difference (+9.9%) was found in the four schools assessed on both sides of that date. The increase in CTL may thus reflect higher scores in recent data rather than a steady progression. Not all indices behaved in the same way: accuracy (CM), which declined before 2018, increased afterwards, including in the four schools, and the reduction in reading time slowed down, whereas the steps in M and C were less robust. Higher CTL in recent years may therefore be carried by more words read correctly and a recovery of accuracy rather than by faster reading. These patterns remain exploratory. There are no data for 2018 and for 2020-2021, and only the 2019 data (40.1% of the sample assessed after 2018) precede the pandemic, so the possible effects of the reforms cannot be separated from those of the school disruptions caused by the pandemic. Moreover, children assessed shortly after 2018 had spent most of their schooling before the reforms, and the observational design, without a comparison group, does not allow higher scores in recent data to be attributed to the reforms or the timing of the apparent change to be established with certainty.

Limitations

The Alouette indices used in this study have their own constraints. In the upper grades, the ceiling of the text limits what M can capture (53% of Grade 5 children read all 265 words), and a sensitivity model with grade-specific slopes suggested that the increase in M may have been weaker in Grades 3 and 5 than in Grade 1, although the global interaction test was not significant. For TL, the censoring at 180 seconds means that the estimated time ratios rely on the model for the many children who did not finish the text (more than 90% in Grades 1 and 2), and the test of grade differences had limited power. The slight decline in accuracy before 2018, concurrent with an increase in the number of words read, may reflect a speed-accuracy trade-off that our data cannot disentangle.

The sample and its structure impose further limits. Because the anonymized data did not allow repeated assessments of the same child across projects to be identified, some observations may not be fully independent. Annual samples (see Appendix A) ranged from 37 to 1,123 children, no data were collected in 2007, 2012, 2014, 2018, 2020 and 2021, and 2019 and 2023 account for 66 2018 is weakly identified. All models except TL included a random intercept for school (ICC = .18 for CTL); for TL, standard errors were made robust to clustering. Yet only 24 of the 49 schools were assessed in more than one year, and only four both before and after 2018, so no random slope for year could be included and the uncertainty around the trend may be underestimated if trends differ across schools. School SPI was available for 2025 only and was treated as time-invariant, so changes in the social composition of schools could not be fully controlled. Finally, the data are cross-sectional, so differences over time reflect differences between successive cohorts rather than changes within children, and the linear trend assumes a constant rate. Although within-school and between-school trends were similar, the representativeness of the samples remains limited, and our results should not be taken as an estimate of changes in reading skills among pupils in general.

Implications and directions for future research

Our results suggest that CTL should not be read as a uniform measure of reading speed. This does not make it uninformative, as shown by its ability to discriminate children with dyslexia, but it argues for interpreting it in light of the child's grade level, for using grade-specific thresholds when it is used for screening, and for reporting it together with its components (reading time, words read, accuracy and the proportion of children who complete the text), which would also help distinguish a slow reader from an inaccurate one. We did not formally test whether the indices measure the same construct on the same scale across grades and years (measurement invariance), so the comparability we question here remains to be examined with dedicated psychometric models.

The higher scores observed in recent data also raise the question of whether reference values derived from earlier samples remain appropriate, including the percentile-based thresholds reported here, which were computed on a corpus pooled over two decades. Answering it would require a dedicated, representative normative study. We wish to stress, however, that periodic re-norming has a limit.

Statistical norms describe where a child stands relative to peers of the same period, so that re-standardizing a test on the contemporary population could make a real decline in performance disappear from view: after a decline, a new standardization restores an apparent norm, and a child may be considered average simply because children of their generation read less well than earlier generations, which says nothing about whether their reading level is satisfactory against an absolute criterion of mastery. School benchmarks, which state the level pupils are expected to reach independently of the current distribution of performance, therefore remain necessary, and the two should be used together: test norms to situate a child relative to peers, and benchmarks to judge mastery. A rise in scores over time and the achievement of a benchmark are distinct questions: although scores were higher in recent data, performance on the Alouette remained below the ministerial benchmarks at every grade (see Section 3.4), with the reservations noted there about comparability.

Future work could use word-level timing (for example, computerized administration) to measure speed in children who do not finish the text, collect test-retest data to estimate reliability and sensitivity to change, model accuracy and speed jointly, validate screening cut-offs in independent samples with predictive values estimated for realistic prevalences, examine how the choice of reference norms (for example, the original 1967 norms or the 2005 revision) changes the proportion of children classified as having reading difficulties over time, and share anonymized data and code so that other teams can test the stability of these findings.

5. CONCLUSION

This study provides extensive descriptive data on Alouette performance across French primary school grades and evidence of its discriminative properties for developmental dyslexia. The contribution of reading speed and accuracy to the efficiency index (CTL) varies depending on whether children complete the text within the allotted time, highlighting the importance of interpreting CTL alongside its components. CTL showed good discriminative performance, although the proposed cut-offs remain exploratory and require independent clinical validation. Reading efficiency was also higher in more recent observations, with an average annual increase of 0.6% across the study period. Exploratory analyses suggested that this difference may be better described by a contrast between observations collected before and after 2018 than by a steady linear trend, but this finding cannot be causally attributed to educational reforms. These findings describe differences between successive cohorts within our samples. They do not constitute a revision of the norms of the test or an estimate of changes in reading skills among pupils in general.
